Computationally Efficient and Statistically Optimal Robust High-Dimensional Linear Regression
Yinan Shen, Jingyang Li, Jian-Feng Cai, Dong Xia

Abstract
High-dimensional linear regression under heavy-tailed noise or outlier corruption is challenging, both computationally and statistically. Convex approaches have been proven statistically optimal but suffer from high computational costs, especially since the robust loss functions are usually non-smooth. More recently, computationally fast non-convex approaches via sub-gradient descent are proposed, which, unfortunately, fail to deliver a statistically consistent estimator even under sub-Gaussian noise. In this paper, we introduce a projected sub-gradient descent algorithm for both the sparse linear regression and low-rank linear regression problems. The algorithm is not only computationally efficient with linear convergence but also statistically optimal, be the noise Gaussian or heavy-tailed with a finite 1 + ε 1+\varepsilon moment. The convergence theory is established for a general framework and its specific applications to absolute loss, Huber loss and quantile loss are investigated. Compared with existing non-convex methods, ours reveals a surprising phenomenon of two-phase convergence . In phase one, the algorithm behaves as in typical non-smooth optimization that requires gradually decaying stepsizes. However, phase one only delivers a statistically sub-optimal estimator, which is already observed in the existing literature. Interestingly, during phase two, the algorithm converges linearly as if minimizing a smooth and strongly convex objective function, and thus a constant stepsize suffices. Underlying the phase-two convergence is the smoothing effect of random noise to the non-smooth robust losses in an area close but not too close to the truth. Numerical simulations confirm our theoretical discovery and showcase the superiority of our algorithm over prior methods.



1
Introduction

Let
\{({\mathbf{X}}_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
be a collection of independent observations satisfying

Y_{i}=\langle{\mathbf{X}}_{i},{\mathbf{T}}^{\ast}\rangle+\xi_{i}

(1)

with
{\mathbf{X}}_{i}
and
Y_{i}
being the
covariate
and
response
, respectively. Here,
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denotes the inner product in Euclidean space. The latent noise
\xi_{i}
is usually assumed
random
for ease of analysis. We assume that
{\mathbf{T}}^{\ast}
resides in a high-dimensional ambient space but has a low-dimensional structure. We focus on two high-dimensional linear regression problems: the
sparse linear regression
where
{\mathbf{T}}^{\ast}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is sparse and
low-rank linear regression
where
{\mathbf{T}}^{\ast}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d}
is low-rank. For both problems, it is of great interest to effectively, both computationally and statistically, recover
{\mathbf{T}}^{\ast}
using as few observations as possible. There is a vast literature discussing the motivations and applications, e.g.,
Tibshirani 1996
;
Needell and Tropp 2009
;
Blumensath and Davies 2009
;
Blumensath and Davies 2010
;
Loh and Wainwright 2011
;
Shen and Li 2017
;
Zhu et al. 2020
;
Zhao et al. 2022
;
Gross et al. 2010
;
Xia and Koltchinskii 2016
;
Chen et al. 2011
;
Negahban and Wainwright 2011
;
Chiu et al. 2021
;
Siddiqi et al. 2010
.

The methods of high-dimensional linear regression can be broadly classified into two categories: the
convex
and
non-convex
approaches.
The typical convex approaches introduce an additional penalization such as the matrix nuclear norm to promote low-rank solutions and the
\ell_{1}
-norm to promote sparsity.
The convexity renders well-understood algorithms for convex programming immediately applicable. Therefore statisticians can just focus on studying the statistical performances without paying too much attention to the computational implementations.
For instance, by assuming the i.i.d. (sub-)
Gaussian
noise with a variance
\sigma^{2}
and the so-called
restricted isometry property
(RIP) or
restricted strong convexity
(RSC) in low-rank linear regression for estimating a
d\times d
matrix,
Candes and Plan 2011
;
Rohde and Tsybakov 2011
;
Negahban and Wainwright 2011
;
Cai and Zhang 2015
;
Chen and Wainwright 2015
;
Davenport and Romberg 2016
;
Chandrasekaran et al. 2012
proved that the nuclear-norm penalized least square estimator attains the error rate
\tilde{O}_{p}(\sigma^{2}rdn^{-1})
1
1

1

Here
\tilde{O}_{p}(\cdot)
stands for the typical big-O notation up to logarithmic factors and holds with high probability.
in the squared Frobenius norm where
r
is the rank. This rate is shown to be optimal in the minimax sense. See, e.g.,
Xia 2014
;
Ma and Wu 2015
and references therein.
Despite the appealing theoretical performances, convex approaches have two major drawbacks. First, convex methods operate directly in the high-dimensional ambient space making them run slowly and are unscalable to ultra-high dimensional problems. Secondly, while the established error rate is theoretically minimax optimal, the implicit constant factor seems large and the resultant error rate is,
in practice
, often inferior to that by non-convex approaches. These issues can be nicely addressed by non-convex approaches. In low-rank linear regression, the non-convex methods could operate on the basis of matrix factorization and resort to the gradient descent type algorithms
(
Chen and Wainwright 2015
;
Burer and Monteiro 2003
;
Zheng and Lafferty 2016
;
Zhao et al. 2015
;
Xia and Yuan 2021
;
Wei et al. 2016
)
; in sparse linear regression, the typical non-convex approaches include the least angel regression
(
Efron et al. 2004
)
, iterative hard thresholding
(
Blumensath and Davies 2009
)
, and best subset selection
(
Zhu et al. 2020
)
, etc. Under similar conditions, it has been demonstrated that these non-convex algorithms converge fast (more exactly, linearly) and also deliver minimax optimal estimators under (sub-)Gaussian noise. We remark that non-convex methods often require more tuning parameters. For instance, the matrix factorization algorithms often require knowing the true rank
(
Chandrasekaran et al. 2012
;
Chen and Wainwright 2015
)
and the iterative hard thresholding algorithms need the information of the underlying sparsity
(
Blumensath and Davies 2009
;
Efron et al. 2004
)
.

The recent boom of data technology poses new challenges to high-dimensional linear regression, among which the heavy-tailed noise and outliers appear routinely in numerous applications such as diffusion-weighted imaging
(
Chang et al. 2005
)
, on-line advertising
(
Sun et al. 2017
)
, and gene-expression data analysis
(
Sun et al. 2020
)
. The aforementioned approaches minimizing a square loss become vulnerable or even completely useless when the noise has a heavy tail or when data are partially corrupted. To mitigate the influence of heavy-tailed noise and corruptions, a natural solution is to replace the square loss by more robust ones. Notable examples include the absolute loss
(
Candès et al. 2011
;
Cambier and Absil 2016
)
, the renowned Huber loss
(
Huber 1965
)
, and quantile loss
(
Koenker and Hallock 2001
)
, all of which are convex but non-smooth.
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
proposed a convex approach based on nuclear-norm penalized absolute or Huber loss for low-rank linear regression, and proved that the estimator attains the error rate
\tilde{O}_{p}(rd_{1}n^{-1})
in squared Frobenius norm as long as the noise has a non-zero density in a neighbourhood of origin. Interestingly,
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
doesn’t need to estimate the noise level for choosing the tuning parameters. A more general framework requiring only Lipschitz and convex loss but imposing a so-called
Bernstein condition
on the noise is investigated by
Alquier et al. 2019
. See also
Klopp et al. 2017
. The foregoing approaches still rely on convex programming and suffer from the issue of computational inefficiency explained above. In fact, the computational issue is severer here with non-smooth objective functions
(
Boyd et al. 2004
)
.

The sub-gradient descent algorithms based on matrix factorization were studied recently by
Charisopoulos et al. 2021
;
Li et al. 2020
;
Tong et al. 2021b
in low-rank linear regression. These algorithms converge linearly if equipped with a good initialization and with properly chosen stepsizes, but the statistical performances of these non-convex approaches under heavy-tailed noise are either largely missing or, generally, sub-optimal. To be more specific,
Li et al. 2020
only proved the exact recovery under sparse outliers
without noise
; the error rates in squared Frobenius norm established in
Charisopoulos et al. 2021
and
Tong et al. 2021b
turn into
\tilde{O}_{p}(\sigma^{2})
even when the noise is Gaussian with variance
\sigma^{2}
, which is not statistically consistent.
There is also a vast literature of sparse linear regression under heavy-tailed noise or partial corruption, e.g.,
Sun et al. 2020
;
Zheng et al. 2015
;
Belloni et al. 2011
;
Alquier et al. 2019
;
Lecué and Mendelson 2017
;
Lugosi and Mendelson 2019b
;
Pan et al. 2021
;
Wang et al. 2021
;
Wang et al. 2020
;
Thompson 2020
. The adpative Huber loss was investigated by
Sun et al. 2020
under a finite
1+\varepsilon
moment condition of noise.
Depersin 2020
studied the median-of-means approach and derived sharp error rates by VC-dimension, achieving the first sub-Gaussian sparse estimators.
Belloni et al. 2011
and
Wang et al. 2020
proposed the square-root LASSO and the absolute loss, respectively. While being statistically robust to heavy tails, these prior works didn’t provide provably fast converging algorithms. In
Foucart and Lecué 2017
, an iterative hard thresholding algorithm was proposed for sparse linear regression by minimizing the absolute loss, which converges fast but a sub-optimal error rate was derived.

In this paper, we propose a computationally efficient non-convex algorithm based on the projected sub-gradient descent for high-dimensional linear regression using robust loss functions. It is essentially the absolute loss hard thresholding algorithm (
IHT
-
\ell_{1}
,
Foucart and Lecué 2017
) for sparse linear regression and the Riemannian sub-gradient descent algorithm (
RsGrad
,
Vandereycken 2013
;
Cambier and Absil 2016
;
Wei et al. 2016
;
Cai et al. 2021a
) for low-rank linear regression. In both problems, the sub-gradient descent algorithm admits fast computation and linear convergence. Unlike
Foucart and Lecué 2017
and
Tong et al. 2021b
, we discover that the sub-gradient descent algorithm exhibits an intriguing phenomenon referred to as the
two-phase convergence
. In phase one, the algorithm behaves like typical
non-smooth
optimization requiring gradually decaying stepsizes through iterations. For example, in low-rank linear regression, if the noise is Gaussian with a variance
\sigma^{2}
and the absolute loss is equipped, the phase-one iterations converge linearly and reach an estimator whose squared Frobenius norm error is
O_{p}(\sigma^{2})
. This rate has been achieved by
Tong et al. 2021b
;
Charisopoulos et al. 2021
whereas theirs is based on a scaled sub-gradient (ScaledSM) descent algorithm. In phase two, the algorithm (RsGrad) behaves like smooth optimization in that a constant stepsize guarantees a linear convergence. As a result, after phase-two iterations, RsGrad can output an estimator which is statistically optimal under mild conditions, e.g. it achieves the rate
O_{p}(\sigma^{2}rdn^{-1})
for low-rank linear regression. The two-phase convergence phenomenon also occurs for sparse linear regression. For both problems, minimax optimal rates can be achieved under heavy-tailed noise.

Our contributions are multi-fold. First, we propose a projected sub-gradient descent algorithm for robust high-dimensional linear regression that is applicable to a wide class of non-smooth loss functions for both the sparse linear regression and low-rank linear regression problems. While the Riemannian sub-gradient descent (RsGrad) has been introduced by
Cambier and Absil 2016
for minimizing the absolute loss, to our best knowledge, there exists no theoretical guarantees for its convergence. Under mild conditions, we prove that the algorithm converges linearly. Secondly, we demonstrate the statistical optimality of the final estimator delivered by our algorithm for both problems, be the noise Gaussian or heavy-tailed. For instance, the specific applications of RsGrad to low-rank linear regression with the absolute loss, Huber loss and quantile loss confirm that a rate
O_{p}(rdn^{-1})
is attainable as long as the noise has a fine
1+\varepsilon
moment and its density satisfies mild regularity conditions. Similar conditions have appeared in
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
. Unlike
Fan et al. 2021
and
Minsker 2018
, our rate is proportional to the noise size. Thirdly, our analysis reveals a new phenomenon of
two-phase convergence
that enables us to achieve the statistically optimal error rates, which is a significant improvement over existing works
(
Foucart and Lecué 2017
;
Li et al. 2020
;
Tong et al. 2021b
;
Charisopoulos et al. 2021
)
. While the phase-one convergence is typical for non-smooth optimization, the phase-two convergence, interestingly, behaves like smooth optimization. It seems that the random noise has an effect of smoothing in the phase-two convergence.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we review three typical examples of non-smooth robust loss functions. We provide a warm-up example of robust sparse linear regression in Section
3
and investigate the two-phase convergence of the iterative hard thresholding algorithm. A general framework of studying the two-phase convergence performance of the Riemannian sub-gradient descent algorithm for robust low-rank linear regression is presented in Section
4
. Non-convex algorithms inevitably involve important tuning parameters, which will be discussed in Section
5
. In Section
6
, we further investigate our methods under the Huber’s contamination model. We showcase the results of numerical experiments and comparison with prior methods in Section
7
. All the proofs are relegated to the Appendix.

2
Robust Loss Functions

Denote
{\mathbb{M}}
the feasible set of the optimization program. It can be the set of matrices with rank bounded by
r
in low-rank regression (Section
4
) and the set of vectors with support size bounded by
\tilde{s}
in sparse regression (Section
3
). The goal of linear regression is to solve

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{T}}:=\underset{{\mathbf{T}}\in{\mathbb{M}}}{\arg\min}\ f({\mathbf{T}})\ \textrm{ where }f({\mathbf{T}}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho\big(\langle{\mathbf{T}},{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\rangle-Y_{i}\big).

(2)

Here
\rho(\cdot):{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is a properly chosen loss function.
The statistical property of
\hat{\mathbf{T}}
crucially relies on the loss function. For instance,
\hat{\mathbf{T}}
attained by using the
\ell_{2}
-loss (
square loss
), i.e.,
\rho(x):=x^{2}
, has been proven effective
(
Chen and Wainwright 2015
;
Wei et al. 2016
;
Xia and Yuan 2021
;
Jain et al. 2014
;
Agarwal et al. 2010
)
in dealing with sub-Gaussian noise but is well-recognized sensitive to outliers and heavy-tailed noise
(
Huber 1965
)
. There are the so-called
robust
loss functions which are relatively immune to outliers and heavy-tailed noise and capable to deliver a more reliable estimate
\hat{\mathbf{T}}
. The examples of robust loss functions include:

1.

Absolute loss (
\ell_{1}
-loss):
\rho(x):=|x|
for any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
;

2.

Huber loss:
\rho_{H,\delta}(x):=x^{2}\mathbbm{1}(|x|\leq\delta)+(2\delta|x|-\delta^{2})\mathbbm{1}(|x|>\delta)
for any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
where
\delta>0
is a tuning parameter;

3.

Quantile loss:
\rho_{Q,\delta}(x):=\delta x\mathbbm{1}(x\geq 0)+(\delta-1)x\mathbbm{1}(x<0)
for any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
with
\delta:={\mathbb{P}}(\xi\leq 0)
.

The absolute loss, Huber loss and quantile loss are all convex functions. They have appeared in the literature of sparse regression including the LASSO-type convex approaches
(
Sun et al. 2020
;
Fan et al. 2021
)
and the non-convex approaches by iterative algorithms
(
Foucart and Lecué 2017
)
. These robust losses have also been investigated for low-rank regression, e.g., the convex approach based on nuclear norm penalization
(
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
;
Klopp et al. 2017
;
Candès et al. 2011
)
and the non-convex approach based on gradient-descent-type algorithms
(
Li et al. 2020
;
Tong et al. 2021a
)
. Compared with the square loss, the aforementioned robust loss functions are non-smooth, i.e., their derivatives are dis-continuous, which brings new challenge to its computation.
Indeed, the optimizing program (
2
) usually exploits the sub-gradient of robust loss functions, which is written as
\partial f({\mathbf{T}})
. See, for instance,
Charisopoulos et al. 2021
and references therein. Recently,
Thompson 2020
showed that regularization using the sorted Huber loss could significantly improve the constant in the error rate than that using the standard Huber loss. However, since the computation of sorted Huber loss is challenging in our framework, we lave it for future work.

The existing statistics literature have proved that
\hat{\mathbf{T}}
attained by using robust loss functions enjoys the statistical robustness to heavy-tailed noise. However, the optimal solution
\hat{\mathbf{T}}
is often solved by heuristic algorithms without convergence guarantees
(
Sun et al. 2020
)
or by non-smooth optimization algorithms
(
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
)
which may converge slowly in general and the computational cost can be considerably high, rendering the applicability of aforementioned robust loss functions questionable for real-world high-dimensional problems. More recently,
Charisopoulos et al. 2021
;
Tong et al. 2021b
;
Foucart and Lecué 2017
investigated sub-gradient descent algorithms for minimizing the robust loss functions showing that these algorithms can converge fast if the stepsizes are properly scheduled along the iterations. Unfortunately, their analysis frameworks all failed to demonstrate that the statistical benefits of robust loss functions claimed by prior statistics literature can be achieved by the sub-gradient descent algorithms. There exists a gap between the statistical and computational efficiencies.

In the subsequent Sections
3
and
4
, we will show that a revised sub-gradient descent algorithm equipped with a novel stepsize schedule converges fast and achieves statistically optimal rates in various settings. Besides the algorithmic design, a novel analysis framework also plays a critical role in developing our statistical theories.

3
Warm-up: Sparse Linear Regression

We begin with a warm-up example of high-dimensional sparse linear regression using the absolute loss. Let
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be sparse with a support size
|\text{supp}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})|=s\ll d
. We observe a collection of independent random pairs
\{({\mathbf{X}}_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
satisfying
Y_{i}=\langle{\mathbf{X}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\rangle+\xi_{i}
where the predictor vector
{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is assumed randomly sampled. Our goal is to estimate
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\ast}
when the noise
\xi_{i}
may have heavy tails. For ease of exposition, we only study the absolute loss
(
Foucart and Lecué 2017
)
but parallel results can be derived for other robust loss functions (see Section
4
). Denote

\displaystyle\hat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}:=\underset{|\text{supp}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})|\leq\tilde{s}}{\arg\min}f({\boldsymbol{\beta}}),\quad\textrm{where }f({\boldsymbol{\beta}}):=\|{\mathbf{X}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\mathbf{Y}}\|_{1}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}|Y_{i}-\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\rangle|.

(3)

Here the chosen sparsity level
\tilde{s}\in[s,d]
,
{\mathbf{X}}=({\mathbf{X}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{X}}_{n})^{\top}
and
{\mathbf{Y}}=(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})^{\top}
. Let
\|\cdot\|_{p}
denote the
\ell_{p}
-norm of vectors for
1\leq p\leq 2
.

3.1
Algorithm: Iterative Hard Thresholding

Our method for optimizing the program (
3
) is essentially the
projected sub-gradient descent
algorithm. The projected gradient descent algorithm has been explored in minimizing the square loss
(
Agarwal et al. 2010
;
Chen and Wainwright 2015
)
, which is easy to implement and fast computable. We resort to the sub-gradient because the absolute loss is non-smooth.

Since projecting a vector onto the set of sparse vectors can be realized by hard thresholding, our algorithm is often referred to as the
Iterative Hard Thresholding

(
Blumensath and Davies 2009
;
Blumensath and Davies 2010
)
. The pseudocodes are presented in Algorithm
1
. The operator
{\mathcal{H}}_{\tilde{s}}({\mathbf{V}})
keeps
{\mathbf{V}}
’s
\tilde{s}
entries with the largest absolute values and zeros out the others. In practice, we choose the sub-gradient
{\mathbf{G}}_{l}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}sign(\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l},{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\rangle-Y_{i}){\mathbf{X}}_{i}
and set
sign(0)=0
. Note that Algorithm
1
allows arbitrary initializations.

Algorithm 1

Iterative Hard Thresholding (
\ell_{1}
-loss)

Input
: observations
\{({\mathbf{X}}_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, max iterations
{l_{\textsf{max}}}
, step sizes
\{\eta_{l}\}_{l=0}^{{l_{\textsf{max}}}}
and sparsity parameter
\tilde{s}
.

Initialization:
arbitrary

{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}
with a support size
|\text{supp}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0})|\leq\tilde{s}

for

l=0,\ldots,{l_{\textsf{max}}}

do

Choose a sub-gradient:
{\mathbf{G}}_{l}\in\partial f({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l})

Truncation:
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l+1}={\mathcal{H}}_{\tilde{s}}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l}-\eta_{l}{\mathbf{G}}_{l})

end

for

Output
:
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{{l_{\textsf{max}}}}

3.2
Theory: two-phase convergence

The convergence performance of Algorithm
1
and statistical property of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
rely on the following two assumptions.

Assumption 1
.

The covariate vectors
{\mathbf{X}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{X}}_{n}
are independent Gaussian
{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\sim N(\boldsymbol{0},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i})
where
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}
’s are symmetric and positive definite. There exist absolute constants
C_{{\tiny l}},C_{{\tiny u}}>0
such that

\displaystyle C_{{\tiny l}}\leq\lambda_{\min}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i})\leq\lambda_{\max}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i})\leq C_{{\tiny u}}.

Assumption 2
.

(Heavy-tailed noise I)
The noise
\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{n}
are i.i.d. with the density
h_{\xi}(\cdot)
and distribution function
H_{\xi}(\cdot)
, respectively.
There exists an
\varepsilon>0
such that
{\mathbb{E}}|\xi|^{1+\varepsilon}<+\infty
. The noise has median zero, i.e.,
H_{\xi}(0)=1/2
. Denote
\gamma={\mathbb{E}}|\xi|
. There exist constants
b_{0},b_{1}>0
(may be dependent on
\gamma
) such that
2
2

2

The lower bound condition can be slightly relaxed to
|H_{\xi}(x)-H_{\xi}(0)|\geq|x|/b_{0}
when
|x|\leq 8(C_{{\tiny u}}/C_{{\tiny l}})^{1/2}\gamma
.

\displaystyle h_{\xi}(x)\geq b_{0}^{-1},

\displaystyle\ \ \ \textrm{ for all }|x|\leq 8(C_{{\tiny u}}/C_{{\tiny l}})^{1/2}\gamma;

\displaystyle h_{\xi}(x)\leq b_{1}^{-1},

\displaystyle\ \ \ \forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}.

By Assumption
2
, a simple fact is
b_{0}\geq 8(C_{{\tiny u}}/C_{{\tiny l}})^{1/2}\gamma
and
b_{0}\geq b_{1}
. We require the noise has a finite
1+\varepsilon
moment, which is essential to guarantee
\lim_{x\to+\infty}x(1-H_{\xi}(x))=0
. This moment condition is fairly weak compared with the existing literature. For instance,
Minsker 2018
;
Depersin 2020
require a finite second-order moment condition;
Fan et al. 2021
imposes a
2+\varepsilon
moment condition. The lower bound on the density function also appeared in
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
and is a special case of the (local) Bernstein condition
(
Alquier et al. 2019
;
Chinot et al. 2020
)
. The upper bound condition on the density function is mild, e.g., a Lipschitz distribution function ensures such a uniform upper bound.

Theorem 1
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
and
2
hold. There exist absolute constants
C_{1},\dots,C_{6}>0
,
c_{2}^{*}\in(0,1)
such that if
n\geq C_{1}C_{{\tiny u}}C_{{\tiny l}}^{-1}\tilde{s}\log(2d/\tilde{s})
,
\tilde{s}\geq C_{2}(C_{{\tiny u}}/C_{{\tiny l}})^{8}(b_{0}/b_{1})^{8}\cdot s
and the initial stepsize
\eta_{0}\in(nC_{{\tiny u}})^{-1}C_{{\tiny l}}^{1/2}\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|_{2}\cdot\left[1/8,\ 3/8\right]
, then with probability over
1-\exp(-C_{3}\tilde{s}\log(2d/\tilde{s}))-3\exp(-(n\log(2d/\tilde{s}))^{1/2}\log^{-1}n)
, the sequence
\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l}\}_{l\leq{l_{\textsf{max}}}}
of Algorithm
1
has the following dynamics:

1.

in phase one, i.e., when
\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|_{2}\geq 8C_{{\tiny l}}^{-1/2}\gamma
, by taking the stepsize
\eta_{l}=(1-c_{1})^{l}\eta_{0}
with any fixed constant
c_{1}\leq C_{{\tiny l}}C_{{\tiny u}}^{-1}/64
, then
\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l+1}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|_{2}\leq(1-c_{1})^{l+1}\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|_{2}
;

2.

in phase two, i.e., when
C_{4}b_{0}C_{{\tiny l}}^{-1}n^{-1/2}\big(C_{{\tiny u}}\tilde{s}\log(2d/\tilde{s})\big)^{1/2}\leq\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|_{2}\leq 8C_{{\tiny l}}^{-1/2}\gamma
, by taking a constant stepsize
\eta_{l}=\eta\in C_{{\tiny l}}^{1/2}b_{1}^{2}(nb_{0}C_{{\tiny u}})^{-1}\cdot\left[C_{5},C_{6}\right]
, then
\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l+1}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|_{2}\leq(1-c_{2}^{*})\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|_{2}

Therefore, after at most
O\big(\log(\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|_{2}/\gamma)+\log(n\gamma b_{0}^{-1}\log(2d/\tilde{s}))\big)
iterations, Algorithm
1
outputs an estimator with the error rate

\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{C_{4}C_{{\tiny u}}}{C_{{\tiny l}}^{2}}\cdot\frac{b_{0}^{2}\tilde{s}\log(2d/\tilde{s})}{n}.

Note that in Theorem
1
if a constant depends on
b_{0}
or/and
b_{1}
, a star sign is placed on its top right corner.
The phase one convergence is typical in non-smooth optimization which requires decaying stepsizes; see also
Foucart and Lecué 2017
. Surprisingly, the phase two convergence requires a constant stepsize as if minimizing a smooth objective function
(
Blumensath and Davies 2009
;
Chen and Wainwright 2015
)
.

Gaussian noise

If the noise is Gaussian
N(0,\sigma^{2})
, we have
b_{0}\asymp b_{1}\asymp\sigma
. If further
C_{{\tiny u}}\asymp C_{{\tiny l}}\asymp 1
, we can choose a sparsity level
\tilde{s}\gtrsim s
so that the output of Algorithm
1
attains the rate
\|\hat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|_{2}^{2}=O_{p}\big(\sigma^{2}\cdot sn^{-1}\log(d/s)\big)
, which is minimax optimal
(
Rigollet and Tsybakov 2011
;
Ye and Zhang 2010
;
Raskutti et al. 2011
;
Lounici et al. 2011
)
.

Heavy-tailed noise

Under a finite second moment condition,
Sun et al. 2020
proved that a Huber-loss-based estimator can achieve the error rate
O\big(s/n\cdot\log d\big)
with probability exceeding
1-d^{-\Omega(1)}
. See also related works in
Pan et al. 2021
;
Wang et al. 2021
;
Wang et al. 2020
. A median-of-means estimator was studied in
Depersin 2020
showing that, under a finite second moment condition, the rate
O\big(s/n\cdot\log(d/s)\big)
can be attained with probability exceeding
1-\Omega(1)\cdot\exp(-s\log(ed/s))
. Our Theorem
1
only requires a finite
1+\varepsilon
moment condition, but the attained error rate is comparable to
Depersin 2020
. Though our estimator is not sub-Gaussian, most existing sub-Gaussian estimators require a finite second moment condition
(
Catoni 2012
;
Lugosi and Mendelson 2019c
;
Lugosi and Mendelson 2019a
;
Hopkins 2020
;
Depersin and Lecué 2022
)
.
Chinot et al. 2020
relaxed the noise moment condition but it is unclear how their estimator can be efficiently computed with theoretical guarantees. More importantly, unlike the aforementioned works, our estimator is attained by a
provably
fast converging algorithm.

Proof sketch of Theorem
1
.

Note that not all the entries of the sub-gradient
{\mathbf{G}}_{l}
affect
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l+1}
. Denote

\Omega_{l+1}:=\text{supp}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l+1}),\quad\Omega_{l}:=\text{supp}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l}),\quad\Pi_{l}:=\text{supp}\big({\mathcal{H}}_{\tilde{s}}({\mathcal{P}}_{\Omega_{l}^{\rm c}}({\mathbf{G}}_{l}))\big),\quad\Omega^{*}:=\text{supp}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}),

where the operator
{\mathcal{P}}_{\Omega}({\mathbf{G}})
keeps the entries
{\mathbf{G}}
in
\Omega
unchanged and zeros out the others and
\Omega^{c}
is complement of
\Omega
. It is clear that
\Omega_{l+1}\subseteq\Omega_{l}\cup\Pi_{l}\subseteq\Omega_{l}\cup\Pi_{l}\cup\Omega^{*}
. We can re-write the update rule as

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l+1}={\mathcal{H}}_{\tilde{s}}\left({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l}-\eta_{l}{\mathbf{G}}_{l}\right)={\mathcal{H}}_{\tilde{s}}\left({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l}-\eta_{l}{\mathcal{P}}_{\Omega_{l}\cup\Pi_{l}\cup\Omega^{*}}({\mathbf{G}}_{l})\right).

It thus suffices to focus on the sparse vector
{\mathcal{P}}_{\Omega_{l}\cup\Pi_{l}\cup\Omega^{*}}({\mathbf{G}}_{l})
. The following lemma depicts the two-phase regularity properties of the loss
f({\boldsymbol{\beta}})
around the oracle
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}
.

Lemma 1
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
and
2
hold. There exist absolute constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},c_{0}>0
such that if
n\geq C_{1}C_{{\tiny u}}C_{{\tiny l}}^{-1}\tilde{s}\log(2d/\tilde{s})
, then with probability over
1-\exp\big(-c_{0}\tilde{s}\log\big(2d/\tilde{s})\big)-3\exp\big(-(n\log(2d/\tilde{s}))^{1/2}\log^{-1}n\big)
, we have

1.

for all
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in\left\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}:\;\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|_{2}\geq 8C_{{\tiny l}}^{-1/2}\gamma,\ |\text{supp}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})|\leq\tilde{s}\right\}
and for all sub-gradient
{\mathbf{G}}\in\partial f({\boldsymbol{\beta}})
,

\displaystyle f({\boldsymbol{\beta}})-f({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})\geq\frac{n}{4}C_{{\tiny l}}^{1/2}\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|_{2}\quad{\rm and}\quad\|{\mathcal{P}}_{\Omega\cup\Pi\cup\Omega^{*}}({\mathbf{G}})\|_{2}\leq nC_{{\tiny u}}^{1/2},

where
\Omega:=\text{supp}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})
and
\Pi:=\text{supp}\big({\mathcal{H}}_{\tilde{s}}({\mathcal{P}}_{\Omega^{\rm c}}({\mathbf{G}}))\big)
;

2.

for all
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in\left\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}:\ C_{2}C_{{\tiny u}}^{1/2}C_{{\tiny l}}^{-1}\cdot b_{0}\big(\tilde{s}/n\cdot\log(2d/\tilde{s})\big)^{1/2}\leq\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|_{2}\leq 8C_{{\tiny l}}^{-1/2}\gamma,\ |\text{supp}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})|\leq\tilde{s}\right\}
and for all sub-gradient
{\mathbf{G}}\in\partial f({\boldsymbol{\beta}})
,

\displaystyle f({\boldsymbol{\beta}})-f({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})\geq\frac{nC_{{\tiny l}}}{12b_{0}}\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\quad{\rm and}\quad\|{\mathcal{P}}_{\Omega\cup\Pi\cup\Omega^{*}}({\mathbf{G}})\|_{2}\leq C_{3}\frac{nC_{{\tiny u}}}{b_{1}}\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|_{2},

where
\Omega:=\text{supp}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})
and
\Pi:=\text{supp}\big({\mathcal{H}}_{\tilde{s}}({\mathcal{P}}_{\Omega^{\rm c}}({\mathbf{G}}))\big)
.

Based on Lemma
1
, we show that during phase one

\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l}-\eta_{l}{\mathcal{P}}_{\Omega_{l}\cup\Pi_{l}\cup\Omega^{\ast}}({\mathbf{G}}_{l})-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\ast}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\ast}\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{n\eta_{l}C_{{\tiny l}}^{1/2}}{2}\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\ast}\|_{2}+n^{2}\eta_{l}^{2}C_{{\tiny u}}.

The selected stepsize guarantees
\eta_{l}\asymp C_{{\tiny l}}^{1/2}C_{{\tiny u}}^{-1}\cdot n^{-1}(1-c_{1})^{l}\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\ast}\|_{2}
, by which the contraction of
\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l+1}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\ast}\|_{2}
is derived using the fact
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l+1}={\mathcal{H}}_{\tilde{s}}\left({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l}-\eta_{l}{\mathcal{P}}_{\Omega_{l}\cup\Pi_{l}\cup\Omega^{*}}({\mathbf{G}}_{l})\right)
. During phase two, by Lemma
1
, we get

\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l}-\eta_{l}{\mathcal{P}}_{\Omega_{l}\cup\Pi_{l}\cup\Omega^{\ast}}({\mathbf{G}}_{l})-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\ast}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\bigg(1-\frac{\eta_{l}nC_{{\tiny l}}}{6b_{0}}+\frac{\eta_{l}^{2}C_{3}^{2}n^{2}C_{{\tiny u}}^{2}}{b_{1}^{2}}\bigg)\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\ast}\|_{2}^{2}.

Similarly, the selected constant stepsize guarantees the contraction of
\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l+1}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\ast}\|_{2}
.
∎

Remark 1

(Smoothing effect of random noise)
.

By Theorem
1
, the phase two convergence of Algorithm
1
requires a constant stepsize as if minimizing a smooth function. Lemma
1
shows that, in the small region around
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\ast}
where the phase two convergence occurs, the loss function
f({\boldsymbol{\beta}})
indeed possesses the regularity conditions which usually appear in smooth optimization, e.g., the square loss
(
Cai et al. 2021a
;
Zhao et al. 2015
)
and logistic loss
(
Lyu et al. 2021
)
. The second phase occurs when the individual random noise starts to (stochastically) dominate
\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{l}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\ast}\|_{2}
. This suggests that, when
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\ast}
is close enough to
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\ast}
, the random noise has the effect of smoothing the objective function. In Figure
1
, we present the shape of the stochastic function
g(t):=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\xi_{i}-t|
and also its sub-differential where
\xi_{i}
’s are i.i.d.
N(0,\tau^{2})
.
The function is clearly non-smooth when
\tau=0
and becomes smoother as
\tau
increases. Randomized smoothing has been observed in optimization and statistics literature. For instance,
Zhang and Xia 2020
proved that the random edge-wise observational error can naturally smooth the Edgeworth expansion of the network moment statistics, whose empirical c.d.f. can be a step function without the smoothing effect. In order to optimize a non-smooth convex function
h({\boldsymbol{\beta}})
, e.g.
\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|_{1}
, with optimal convergence rates,
Duchi et al. 2012
proposed to optimize the randomly-smoothed version
{\mathbb{E}}h({\boldsymbol{\beta}}+Z)
where
Z
is some random variable with a known distribution.

(a)

Value of
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\xi_{i}-t|
against
t

(b)

Value of
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\text{sign}(t-\xi_{i})
against
t

Figure 1
:
Sample size
n=1000
and
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
follow
N(0,0.1^{2})
,
N(0,1)
,
N(0,10^{2})
respectively.

4
Low-rank Linear Regression

Let
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
be a
d_{1}\times d_{2}
matrix with rank
r\ll d_{2}
. Without loss of generality, we assume
d_{1}\geq d_{2}
. Denote
\{({\mathbf{X}}_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
the collection of i.i.d. observations satisfying
Y_{i}=\langle{\mathbf{X}}_{i},{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\rangle+\xi_{i}:={\rm tr}({\mathbf{X}}_{i}^{\top}{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast})+\xi_{i}

with
{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}
known as the
measurement matrix
. Our goal is to estimate
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
by solving the optimization program

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{M}}:=\underset{{\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{r}}{\arg\min}\ f({\mathbf{M}})\quad\textrm{ where }f({\mathbf{M}}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho\big(\langle{\mathbf{M}},{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\rangle-Y_{i}\big),

(4)

where
{\mathbb{M}}_{r}:=\{{\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}:\;\text{rank}({\mathbf{M}})\leq r\}
. Here
\rho(\cdot)
is a general robust loss function including the absolute loss
(
Tong et al. 2021b
;
Charisopoulos et al. 2021
)
, Huber loss
(
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
;
Sun et al. 2020
)
and quantile loss
(
Alquier et al. 2019
;
Chen et al. 2022
)
.

4.1
Algorithm: Riemannian optimization

Our algorithm is the projected sub-gradient descent on the Riemannian manifold (RsGrad). At the
l
-th iteration with a current low-rank estimate
{\mathbf{M}}_{l}
, the algorithm consists of two major steps. It begins with computing the Riemannian sub-gradient, which is the projection of a
vanilla
sub-gradient
{\mathbf{G}}_{l}\in\partial f({\mathbf{M}}_{l})\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}
onto the tangent space of
{\mathbb{M}}_{r}
at the point
{\mathbf{M}}_{l}
, denoted by
{\mathbb{T}}_{l}
. The second step is to update the low-rank estimate along the direction of negative Riemannian sub-gradient and then retract it back to the manifold
{\mathbb{M}}_{r}
, for which, it suffices to take the SVD. Here
{\rm SVD}_{r}(\cdot)
returns the best rank-
r
approximation by SVD. The details can be found in Algorithm
2
.

We note that the Riemannian sub-gradient is used only for the benefit of computational efficiency. The theoretical results all hold if taking the vanilla sub-gradient. The detailed computation of Riemannian sub-gradient can be found in the supplementary file. RsGrad has its origin in
Cambier and Absil 2016
for minimizing the absolute loss without convergence and statistical analysis. Our framework covers general robust loss functions, and we prove its computational efficiency and statistical optimality in several important applications.

Comparison with existing algorithms

Sub-gradient descent algorithms have been investigated by
Tong et al. 2021b
;
Charisopoulos et al. 2021
for low-rank linear regression with the absolute loss. Our algorithm is different in two aspects: we take Riemannian sub-gradient and have two phases of stepsize schedule. Factorization-based optimizations
(
Zheng and Lafferty 2016
;
Zhao et al. 2015
)
reparametrize the program (
4
) by
{\mathbf{M}}={\mathbf{U}}{\mathbf{V}}^{\top}
. Then it suffices to update
({\mathbf{U}},{\mathbf{V}})
sequentially to minimize (
2
) by sub-gradient descent algorithms. These algorithms can suffer a great loss of computational efficiency if
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
is
ill-conditioned
. Though the issue can be theoretically remedied by a proper inverse scaling
(
Tong et al. 2021a
)
, it can cause a potential computational instability especially when
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
has small non-zero singular values. Recently, it is discovered that optimizing (
2
) directly on the low-rank manifold
{\mathbb{M}}_{r}
, called
Riemannian
, enjoys the fast computational speed of factorization-based approaches and, meanwhile, converges linearly regardless of the condition number of
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
. See, e.g.,
Cai et al. 2021a
and
Cai et al. 2021b
, for the convergence of Riemannian gradient descent (RGrad) algorithms in minimizing
strongly convex and smooth
functions with tensor-related applications. We note that RGrad is similar to the projected gradient descent (PGD,
Chen and Wainwright 2015
) except that RGrad utilizes the Riemannian gradient while PGD takes the vanilla one.

Algorithm 2

Riemannian Sub-gradient Descent (RsGrad)

Input
: observations
\{({\mathbf{X}}_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, max iterations
{l_{\textsf{max}}}
, step sizes
\{\eta_{l}\}_{l=0}^{{l_{\textsf{max}}}}
.

Initialization:
{\mathbf{M}}_{0}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{r}

for

l=0,\ldots,{l_{\textsf{max}}}

do

Choose a vanilla subgradient:
{\mathbf{G}}_{l}\in\partial f({\mathbf{M}}_{l})

Compute the Riemannian sub-gradient:
\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}_{l}={\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathbb{T}}_{l}}({\mathbf{G}}_{l})

Retraction to
{\mathbb{M}}_{r}
:
{\mathbf{M}}_{l+1}=\text{SVD}_{r}({\mathbf{M}}_{l}-\eta_{l}\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}_{l})

end

for

Output
:
\hat{\mathbf{M}}={\mathbf{M}}_{{l_{\textsf{max}}}}

4.2
General Convergence Performance

We now present the general convergence performance of RsGrad Algorithm
2
, which essentially relies on the regularity conditions of the objective function. These conditions, in spirit, largely inherit those from existing literature
(
Charisopoulos et al. 2021
;
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
;
Alquier et al. 2019
;
Tong et al. 2021a
)
. However, as explained in Section
1
, these prior works only delivered statistically sub-optimal estimates. It turns out that more delicate characterizations of these conditions are necessary for our purpose. More exactly, we discover that the aforementioned robust functions exhibit strikingly different regularity conditions near and far away from
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
, referred to as the
two-phase
regularity conditions. Here
\|\cdot\|_{\rm F}
represents the Frobenius norm of a matrix.

Condition 1
.

(Two-phase regularity conditions)
Define the two regions around
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
with
\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}>\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}>0
by

{\mathbb{B}}_{1}:=\left\{{\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{r}:\|{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\geq\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}\right\}\quad{\rm and}\quad{\mathbb{B}}_{2}:=\left\{{\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{r}:\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}>\|{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\geq\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}\right\}.

The function
f(\cdot):{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
satisfies

1.

the rank-
r
restricted
(\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}},\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}},\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}},\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}})

two-phase sharpness
with respect to
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
,

f({\mathbf{M}})-f({\mathbf{M}}^{\ast})\geq\begin{cases}\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}\|{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F},&\textrm{ for }\ \ {\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{B}}_{1};\\
\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}\|{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2},&\textrm{ for }\ \ {\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{B}}_{2};\end{cases}

2.

the rank-r restricted
(\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}},\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}},L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}},L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}})

two-phase sub-gradient bound
with respect to
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
,

\|{\mathbf{G}}\|_{\rm F,r}\leq\begin{cases}L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}},&\textrm{ for }\ \ {\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{B}}_{1};\\
L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}\|{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F},&\textrm{ for }\ \ {\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathbb{B}}_{2},\end{cases}

where
{\mathbf{G}}\in\partial f({\mathbf{M}})
and the truncated Frobenius norm
\|{\mathbf{G}}\|_{\rm F,r}:=\|{\rm SVD}_{r}({\mathbf{G}})\|_{\rm F}
.

Basically, Condition
1
dictates the distinct behaviors of
f(\cdot)
in two neighbourhoods of
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
. The quantity
\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}
reflects the statistical limit while
\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}
is usually the rate achieved by the computational analysis in existing literature
(
Tong et al. 2021a
;
Charisopoulos et al. 2021
)
without assuming noise distributions. These prior works only reveal the first phase regularity conditions, i.e., on
{\mathbb{B}}_{1}
. Oftentimes, we have
\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}\gg\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}
, in which case these prior works only deliver statistically sub-optimal estimates. Deriving the second phase regularity condition is challenging where more precise calculations are necessary. We remark that, in statistics literature, the sub-gradient bound is related to the Lipschitz continuity
(
Alquier et al. 2019
)
and the sharpness condition is called the
one-point-margin condition

(
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
)
. However, their estimators are built upon convex program so that the analysis is only made in a small neighbour of
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
where the two-phase regularity conditions are not pivotal.
We summarize the two-phase regularity conditions for various applications in Table
1
. Proposition
1
characterizes the general dynamic of Algorithm
2
.

Loss function

Noise

\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}

\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}

\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}

L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}

\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}

L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}

\ell_{1}
-Loss

N(0,\sigma^{2})

\sigma

\frac{n}{12}

2n

O\left(\frac{n}{\sigma}\right)

\ell_{1}
-Loss

8\gamma

O\left(b_{0}\left(\frac{rd_{1}}{n}\right)^{1/2}\right)

\frac{n}{12b_{0}}

\rho_{H,\delta}

Heavy-tailed

\rho_{Q,\delta}

Table 1
:
Summary of the two phase regularity conditions. In fact,
\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}
is the error rate achieved after the phase one iterations and
\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}
is the error rate achieved after phase two iterations, i.e., the ultimate statistical accuracy. Here
\gamma,b_{0}
and
b_{1}
are as described in Assumption
2
. Note that
b_{0}\asymp\gamma
in many cases such as the Gaussian noise, zero symmetric Pareto noise, and Student’s t noise, etc.
By viewing
b_{0}/\gamma
as a constant, we have
\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}\ll\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}
if the sample size is large.

Proposition 1
.

Suppose Condition
1
holds, the initialization
{\mathbf{M}}_{0}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{r}
satisfies
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{0}\sigma_{r}({\mathbf{M}}^{\ast})\cdot\min\{\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}^{2}L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}^{-2},\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{2}L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{-2}\}
for a small but absolute constant
c_{0}>0
, and the initial stepsize
\eta_{0}\in\left[0.2\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{-2},\ 0.3\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{-2}\right]
. At the
l
-th iteration of Algorithm
2
,

1.

when
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\geq\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}
, namely in phase one, take the stepsize
\eta_{l}=\big(1-c\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{2}L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{-2}\big)^{l}\eta_{0}
with any fixed constant
c\leq 0.04
, then we have

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l+1}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\left(1-c\frac{\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{2}}{L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}^{2}}\right)^{l+1}\cdot\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm F};

2.

when
\tau_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}>\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\geq\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}
, namely in phase two, take the stepsize
\eta_{l}=\eta\in\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}^{-2}\cdot\big[0.125,\ 0.75\big]
, then we have

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l+1}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq\left(1-\frac{\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}^{2}}{32L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}^{2}}\right)\cdot\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}.

By Proposition
1
, the RsGrad Algorithm
2
converges linearly using the proposed stepsizes and outputs an estimate within an
O(\tau_{\textsf{\tiny stat}})
distance in Frobenius norm from the oracle. As discussed above, the phase one convergence is typical in non-smooth optimization, for which the decaying stepsizes are needed. For simplicity, we apply the geometrically decaying stepsizes
(
Tong et al. 2021a
;
Goffin 1977
)
. The phase two convergence behaves like smooth optimization so that a fixed stepsize suffices to yield the linear convergence. Note that the convergence dynamic is free of the matrix condition number, which is a benefit of Riemannian-type algorithms.

Comparison with prior works

The algorithmic dynamic of RsGrad in phase one is similar to that in
Charisopoulos et al. 2021
and
Tong et al. 2021b
, where the geometrically decaying stepsizes in phase one will eventually bring the stepsize into the level
\Omega(\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}^{-2})
which is desired by the phase-two convergence. However,
Charisopoulos et al. 2021
and
Tong et al. 2021b
will continue to shrink the stepsize geometrically and fail to deliver a statistically optimal estimator. In contrast, we set a constant stepsize in phase two and the ultimate estimator is statistically optimal. The difference is observed in numerical experiments in Section
7
.

4.3
Applications

This section presents the statistical performances of RsGrad in some concrete examples. Due to page limit, some examples are deferred to the supplementary file. We note that, compared with the square loss
(
Xia et al. 2021
;
Zhao et al. 2015
;
Chen and Wainwright 2015
)
, low-rank regression using the robust loss functions requires no signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) conditions (except for initialization).

4.3.1
Absolute loss with Gaussian noise

Though motivated by heavy-tailed noise, to start with, we demonstrate that the absolute loss can yield a statistically optimal estimator under Gaussian noise. The objective function is
f({\mathbf{M}})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}|Y_{i}-\langle{\mathbf{M}},{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\rangle|
.

Theorem 2
.

Assume
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}N(0,\sigma^{2})
and
\{{\rm vec}({\mathbf{X}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
satisfy Assumption
1
. There exist absolute constants
c_{0},c_{1},c_{2}\in(0,1),C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that if the sample size
n\geq C_{1}C_{{\tiny u}}C_{{\tiny l}}^{-1}d_{1}r
, the initialization
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{0}\sigma_{r}
for
{\mathbf{M}}_{0}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{r}
, the initial stepsize
\eta_{0}\in C_{{\tiny l}}^{1/2}(48nC_{{\tiny u}})^{-1}\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\cdot\left[0.2,0.3\right]
, the phase one stepsize
\eta_{l}=(1-c_{3})^{l}\eta_{0}
with any fixed constant
c_{3}\leq c_{1}C_{{\tiny l}}C_{{\tiny u}}^{-1}
, and the phase two stepsize
\eta_{l}=\eta\asymp C_{{\tiny l}}C_{{\tiny u}}^{-2}\sigma/n
, then with probability at least
1-\exp(-c_{2}rd_{1})-3\exp(-\sqrt{n}/\log n)
, after at most
O\big(\log(\sigma_{r}/\sigma)+\log(n/d_{1})\big)
iterations, the Algorithm
2
outputs an estimator with the error rate

\|\hat{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq C_{2}\frac{C_{{\tiny u}}}{C_{{\tiny l}}^{2}}\cdot\frac{\sigma^{2}d_{1}r}{n}.

Note that the initial stepsize
\eta_{0}
can be as large as
O(\sigma_{r}n^{-1})
depending on the initialization. The attained error rate is minimax optimal
(
Ma and Wu 2015
;
Xia 2014
)
. In comparison, the algorithms proposed in
Tong et al. 2021b
;
Charisopoulos et al. 2021
and their analysis only achieved the sub-optimal rate
O_{p}(\sigma^{2})
. The proof of Theorem
2
is a combination of the Proposition
1
and the two-phase regularity conditions of
f({\mathbf{M}})
. The two phase convergence dynamics are observed in numerical experiments in Section
7
.

4.3.2
Absolute loss with heavy-tailed noise

We now demonstrate the effectiveness of the absolute loss in handling heavy-tailed noise. Note that in Theorem
3
if a constant factor depends on
b_{0}
or/and
b_{1}
, a star sign is placed on top right of it.

Theorem 3
.

Assume
\{{\rm vec}({\mathbf{X}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
satisfy Assumptions
1
and
2
, respectively. There exist constants
c_{0}^{\ast},c_{1},c_{2}\in(0,1),C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that if the sample size
n\geq C_{1}C_{{\tiny u}}C_{{\tiny l}}^{-1}d_{1}r
, the initialization
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{0}^{\ast}\sigma_{r}
for
{\mathbf{M}}_{0}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{r}
, the initial stepsize
\eta_{0}\in C_{{\tiny l}}^{1/2}(16nC_{{\tiny u}})^{-1}\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\cdot\left[0.2,0.3\right]
, the phase one stepsize
\eta_{l}=(1-c_{3})^{l}\eta_{0}
with any fixed constant
c_{3}\leq c_{1}
, and the phase two stepsize
\eta_{l}=\eta\asymp C_{{\tiny l}}C_{{\tiny u}}^{-2}b_{1}^{2}(nb_{0})^{-1}
, then with probability at least
1-\exp(-c_{2}rd_{1})-3\exp(-\sqrt{n}/\log n)
, after at most
O\big(\log(\sigma_{r}/\gamma)+\log\big(\gamma n/(d_{1}rb_{0})\big)\big)
iterations, the Algorithm
2
outputs an estimator with the error rate

\|\hat{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq\frac{C_{2}C_{{\tiny u}}}{C_{{\tiny l}}^{2}}\cdot\frac{b_{0}^{2}d_{1}r}{n}.

The proof of Theorem
3
is an immediate consequence of Proposition
1
if we have the two-phase regularity conditions on
f({\mathbf{M}})
under heavy-tailed noise, for which we borrowed a powerful tool of bounding an empirical process
(
Adamczak 2008
;
Van de Geer and van de Geer 2000
)
. This allows us to derive a stronger tail probability bound by avoiding bounding the term
\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\xi_{i}|
. Indeed, since
\xi
has a finite
1+\varepsilon
moment, bounding the sum of
|\xi_{i}|
results into a tail probability
1-O(n^{-\varepsilon})
. This is not a desirable tail probability for a small
\varepsilon
.
Note that the Gaussian noise also satisfies Assumption
2
, in which case the attained rate in Theorem
3
has a matching minimax lower bound. The rate is minimax optimal w.r.t. the dimension and sample size. Note that
b_{0}\asymp\gamma={\mathbb{E}}|\xi|
in many cases such as the Gaussian, zero symmetric Pareto, and Student’s t noise.

The density requirement in Assumption
2
is similar to the one in
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
whose estimator is based on the nuclear-norm penalized convex program without considering the computational efficiency. The same error rate
O(b_{0}^{2}d_{1}r/n)
was also derived there. But our approach computes fast and requires only a logarithmic factor of iterations. The analysis frameworks in
Tong et al. 2021b
;
Charisopoulos et al. 2021
would produce the sub-optimal rate
O(\gamma^{2})
under the heavy-tailed noise, which won’t decrease as the sample size
n
gets larger. The error rates derived by
Fan et al. 2021
;
Minsker 2015
and
Minsker 2018
were not proportional to the noise level and were not zero even if the observations are noiseless. In contrast, our algorithm can exactly recover
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
when there is no noise.

4.3.3
Huber loss with heavy-tailed noise

Huber loss is prevalent in robust statistics
(
Huber 1965
;
Sun et al. 2020
;
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
)
and defined by
\rho_{H,\delta}(x):=x^{2}\mathbbm{1}(|x|\leq\delta)+(2\delta|x|-\delta^{2})\mathbbm{1}(|x|>\delta)
where
\delta>0
is referred to as the robustification parameter. The function
\rho_{H,\delta}(\cdot)
is Lipschitz with a constant
2\delta
. The loss function is given by
f({\mathbf{M}}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{H,\delta}(Y_{i}-\langle{\mathbf{M}},{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\rangle)
. Due to technical issues, we need a slightly different assumption of the heavy-tailed noise.

Assumption 3
.

(Heavy-tailed noise II)
There exists an
\varepsilon>0
such that
{\mathbb{E}}|\xi|^{1+\varepsilon}<+\infty
. The noise distribution function
H_{\xi}(\cdot)
is symmetric
3
3

3

The symmetric condition can also be replaced by
\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}H_{\xi}(x)\,dx=\delta
.
in that
H_{\xi}(x)=1-H_{\xi}(-x)
. Denote
\gamma={\mathbb{E}}|\xi|
. There exist constants
b_{0},b_{1}
(may be dependent on
\gamma
and
\delta
) such that

\displaystyle H_{\xi}(x+\delta)-H_{\xi}(x-\delta)\geq 2\delta b_{0}^{-1},

\displaystyle\ \ \ \textrm{ for all }|x|\leq 8(C_{{\tiny u}}/C_{{\tiny l}})^{1/2}\gamma+2(C_{{\tiny u}}/C_{{\tiny l}})^{1/2}\delta;

\displaystyle H_{\xi}(x+\delta)-H_{\xi}(x-\delta)\leq 2\delta b_{1}^{-1},

\displaystyle\ \ \ \forall x\in{\mathbb{R}},

where
\delta
is the Huber loss parameter.

Theorem
4
describes the convergence and statistical performance of RsGrad Algorithm
2
for Huber loss. Similarly, the constants dependent on
b_{0}
or/and
b_{1}
are marked with a star.

Theorem 4
.

Assume
\{{\rm vec}({\mathbf{X}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
satisfy Assumptions
1
and
3
, respectively. There exist constants
c_{0}^{\ast},c_{1},c_{2}\in(0,1),C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that if the sample size
n\geq C_{1}C_{{\tiny u}}C_{{\tiny l}}^{-1}d_{1}r
, the initialization
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{0}^{\ast}\sigma_{r}
for
{\mathbf{M}}_{0}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{r}
, the initial stepsize
\eta_{0}\in C_{{\tiny l}}^{1/2}(32n\delta C_{{\tiny u}})^{-1}\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\cdot\left[0.2,0.3\right]
, the phase one stepsize
\eta_{l}=(1-c_{3})^{l}\eta_{0}
with any fixed constant
c_{3}\leq c_{1}
, and the phase two stepsize
\eta_{l}=\eta\asymp C_{{\tiny l}}C_{{\tiny u}}^{-2}b_{1}^{2}(n\delta b_{0})^{-1}
, then with probability at least
1-\exp(-c_{2}rd_{1})-3\exp(-\sqrt{n}/\log n)
, after at most
O\big(\log(\sigma_{r}/\gamma)+\log\big(\gamma n/(b_{0}d_{1}r)\big)\big)
iterations, the Algorithm
2
outputs an estimator with the error rate

\|\hat{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq\frac{C_{2}C_{{\tiny u}}}{C_{{\tiny l}}^{2}}\cdot\frac{b_{0}^{2}d_{1}r}{n}.

Note that Assumption
3
implies that
b_{0}
is lower bounded by
\Omega(\gamma+\delta)
and thus the error rate is lower bounded by
\Omega\big((\delta^{2}+\gamma^{2})rd_{1}/n\big)
. This suggests an interesting transition with respect to the robustification parameter: when
\delta\lesssim\gamma
, increasing
\delta
does not affect the error rate; when
\delta\gtrsim\gamma
, the error rate appreciates if
\delta
becomes larger.
Sun et al. 2020
derived the statistical performance of Huber loss for linear regression without imposing regularity conditions on the noise density resulting into
an error rate that is slower than
O(n^{-1})
.

4.3.4
Quantile loss with heavy-tailed noise

Quantile loss was initially proposed by
Koenker and Bassett Jr 1978
and has been a popular loss function for robust statistics
(
Welsh 1989
;
Koenker and Hallock 2001
;
Wang et al. 2012
;
Tan et al. 2022
)
. Denote the quantile loss by
\rho_{Q,\delta}:=\delta x\mathbbm{1}(x\geq 0)+(\delta-1)x\mathbbm{1}(x<0)
for any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
with
\delta:={\mathbb{P}}(\xi\leq 0)
. Notice that the function
\rho_{Q,\delta}(\cdot)
is Lipschitz with a constant
\max\{\delta,1-\delta\}
. The loss function is given by
f({\mathbf{M}}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{Q,\delta}(Y_{i}-\langle{\mathbf{M}},{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\rangle)
. Similar to the case of Huber loss, due to technical reasons, our analysis framework needs a slightly different assumption on the heavy-tailed noise.

Assumption 4
.

(Heavy-tailed noise III)
There exists an
\varepsilon>0
such that
{\mathbb{E}}|\xi|^{1+\varepsilon}<+\infty
. Suppose
\delta:=H_{\xi}(0)
lies in
(0,1)
. Denote
\gamma={\mathbb{E}}|\xi|
. There exist constants
b_{0},b_{1}>0
(may be dependent on
\gamma
) such that

\displaystyle h_{\xi}(x)\geq b_{0}^{-1},

\displaystyle\ \ \ \textrm{ for all }|x|\leq 8(C_{{\tiny u}}/C_{{\tiny l}})^{1/2}\gamma;

\displaystyle h_{\xi}(x)\leq b_{1}^{-1},

\displaystyle\ \ \ \forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}.

When the noise has a zero median, namely
\delta=1/2
, Assumption
4
becomes identical to the Assumption
2
.

Theorem 5
.

Assume
\{\text{vec}({\mathbf{X}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
satisfy Assumptions
1
and
4
, respectively. There exist constants
c_{0}^{\ast},c_{1},c_{2}\in(0,1),C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that if sample size
n\geq C_{1}C_{{\tiny u}}C_{{\tiny l}}^{-1}d_{1}r
, the initialization
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{0}^{\ast}\sigma_{r}
, the initial stepsize
\eta_{0}\in C_{{\tiny l}}^{1/2}(8nC_{{\tiny u}})^{-1}\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\cdot[0.2,0.3]
, the phase one stepsize
\eta_{l}=(1-c_{3})^{l}\eta_{0}
with any fixed constant
c_{3}\leq c_{1}
, and the phase two stepsize
\eta_{l}=\eta\asymp C_{{\tiny l}}C_{{\tiny u}}^{-2}b_{1}^{2}(nb_{0})^{-1}
, then with probability over
1-\exp(-c_{2}rd_{1})-3\exp(-\sqrt{n}/\log n)
, after at most
O\big(\log(\sigma_{r}/\gamma)+\log\big(\max\{\delta^{-1},(1-\delta)^{-1}\}\cdot\gamma n/(d_{1}rb_{0})\big)\big)
iterations, the Algorithm
2
outputs an estimator with the error rate

\|\hat{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq\frac{C_{2}C_{{\tiny u}}\cdot\max\{\delta^{2},\ (1-\delta)^{2}\}}{C_{{\tiny l}}^{2}}\cdot\frac{b_{0}^{2}rd_{1}}{n}.

5
Initialization and Algorithmic Parameters Selection

Initialization

The sparse linear regression Algorithm
1
allows arbitrary initialization. We only discuss the initialization for low-rank linear regression.
The convergence of Algorithm
2
crucially relies on the warm initialization
{\mathbf{M}}_{0}
. One can simply apply the shrinkage low-rank approximation
(
Fan et al. 2021
)
for initialization but a finite
2+\varepsilon
moment condition on noise is required. For simplicity, we investigate the performance of the vanilla low-rank approximation. Here
vec(\cdot)
and
mat(\cdot)
denote the vectorization and matricization operators, respectively.

Theorem 6
.

(Initialization Guarantees) Suppose
\gamma_{1}:={\mathbb{E}}|\xi|^{1+\varepsilon}<+\infty
for some
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
and
\{vec({\mathbf{X}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
satisfy Assumption
1
with known covariances
\{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
.
Initialize by

{\mathbf{M}}_{0}:=\operatorname{SVD}_{r}(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\text{mat}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}^{-1}\text{vec}({\mathbf{X}}_{i}))Y_{i}).

For any small
c_{0}>0
, there exist constants
C,c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
depending only on
c_{0}
such that if
n>C\max\big\{C_{{\tiny u}}C_{{\tiny l}}^{-1}\kappa^{2}d_{1}r^{2}\log d_{1},\allowbreak(C_{{\tiny l}}^{-1}d_{1}r)^{(1+\varepsilon)/(2\varepsilon)}\sigma_{r}^{-(1+\varepsilon)/\varepsilon}(\gamma_{1}\log d_{1})^{1/\varepsilon}\big\}
, then with probability over
1-c_{1}d_{1}^{-1}-c_{2}\log^{-1}d_{1}\allowbreak-c_{3}e^{-d_{1}}
, the initialization satisfies
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{0}\sigma_{r}
. Here
\kappa:=\sigma_{1}({\mathbf{M}}^{\ast})\sigma_{r}^{-1}({\mathbf{M}}^{\ast})
is the condition number.

Compared with
Fan et al. 2021
, Theorem
6
only requires a finite
1+\varepsilon
moment condition.
We note that if the noise term
\xi_{i}
has a finite
2+\varepsilon
moment, the probability term
O(\log^{-1}d_{1})
can be replaced by
O(n^{-\min\{1,\varepsilon/2\}})
. Moreover, with a finite
2+\varepsilon
moment, one can even obtain a sub-Gaussian initial estimator by the median-of-means
(
Minsker 2015
;
Lugosi and Mendelson 2019c
;
Lugosi and Mendelson 2019b
)
or the Catoni’s estimator
(
Catoni 2012
;
Minsker 2018
)
. Since the shrinkage-based approaches
(
Fan et al. 2021
;
Minsker 2018
)
are already effective to provide warm initializations, we spare no additional efforts to improve Theorem
6
. If the covariances
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}
’s are unknown, we suggest to apply
Fan et al. 2021
or
Minsker 2018
for obtaining the initialization.

Selection of initial stepsize

Though the main theorems in Sections
3
and
4
require the initial stepsize
\eta_{0}\asymp n^{-1}\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\ast}\|_{2}
and
\eta_{0}\asymp n^{-1}\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}
, respectively, the following theorems show that similar results can be attained even if we only have an upper bound of
\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\ast}\|_{2}
and
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}
.

Theorem
1
*

(alternative of Theorem
1
)
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
and
2
hold. There exist absolute constants
C_{1},\dots,C_{6}>0
,
c_{2}^{*}\in(0,1)
such that if
n\geq C_{1}C_{{\tiny u}}C_{{\tiny l}}^{-1}\tilde{s}\log(2d/\tilde{s})
,
\tilde{s}\geq C_{2}(C_{{\tiny u}}/C_{{\tiny l}})^{8}(b_{0}/b_{1})^{8}\cdot s
, the initial stepsize
\eta_{0}\in(nC_{{\tiny u}})^{-1}C_{{\tiny l}}^{1/2}D_{0}\cdot\left[1/8,\ 3/8\right]
for any
D_{0}\geq\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\ast}\|_{2}
, the phase-one stepsize
\eta_{l}=(1-c_{1})^{l}\eta_{0}
for any
c_{1}\leq C_{{\tiny l}}C_{{\tiny u}}^{-1}/64
, and the phase-two stepsize
\eta_{l}=\eta\in C_{{\tiny l}}^{1/2}b_{1}^{2}(nb_{0}C_{{\tiny u}})^{-1}\cdot[C_{3},\ C_{4}]
, then with probability exceeding
1-\exp\big(-C_{5}\tilde{s}\log(2d/\tilde{s})\big)-3\big(-(n\log(2d/\tilde{s})^{1/2}\log^{-1}n\big)
, after at most
O\big(\log(D_{0}/\gamma)+\log(n\gamma b_{0}^{-1}\log(2d/\tilde{s}))\big)
iterations, the Algorithm
1
outputs an estimator with the error rate

\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\ast}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{C_{6}C_{{\tiny u}}}{C_{{\tiny l}}^{2}}\cdot\frac{b_{0}^{2}\tilde{s}\log(2d/\tilde{s})}{n}.

Theorem
3
*

(alternative of Theorem
3
)
.

Assume
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
\{{\rm vec}({\mathbf{X}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
satisfy Assumptions
1
and
2
, respectively. There exist constants
c_{0}^{\ast},c_{1},c_{2}\in(0,1),C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that if
n\geq C_{1}C_{{\tiny u}}C_{{\tiny l}}^{-1}d_{1}r
,
the initialization
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq D_{0}
for any
D_{0}\leq c_{0}^{\ast}\sigma_{r}
, the initial stepsize
\eta_{0}\in C_{{\tiny l}}^{1/2}(16nC_{{\tiny u}})^{-1}D_{0}\cdot\left[0.2,0.3\right]
, the phase one stepsize
\eta_{l}=(1-c_{3})^{l}\eta_{0}
with any fixed constant
c_{3}\leq c_{1}
, and the phase two stepsize
\eta_{l}=\eta\asymp C_{{\tiny l}}C_{{\tiny u}}^{-2}b_{1}^{2}(nb_{0})^{-1}
, then with probability at least
1-\exp(-c_{2}rd_{1})-3\exp(-n^{1/2}/\log n)
, after at most
O\big(\log(D_{0}/\gamma)+\log\big(\gamma n/(d_{1}rb_{0})\big)\big)
iterations, the Algorithm
2
outputs an estimator with the error rate

\|\hat{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq\frac{C_{2}C_{{\tiny u}}}{C_{{\tiny l}}^{2}}\cdot\frac{b_{0}^{2}d_{1}r}{n}.

By Theorem
*
‣
5
, it thus suffices to obtain an estimate of
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}
. Towards that end, one appropriate method is to take the average
n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|Y_{i}-\langle{\mathbf{M}}_{0},{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\rangle|
, which, under mild conditions, is of the same order of
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}
with high probability. The same approach is also applicable in sparse vector regression. The general quantities
\mu_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}
and
L_{\textsf{\tiny comp}}
involved in Algorithm
2
are often
\asymp n
up to a factor of loss-related parameters, which can be easily tuned in practice.

Tuning Parameters in RsGrad

Our algorithm assumes knowing the rank of
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
, which is typical in non-convex methods
(
Tong et al. 2021a
;
Chen and Wainwright 2015
;
Cai et al. 2021a
)
. One may apply the BIC or AIC for the selection of the rank
(
Cai et al. 2021a
)
. While we believe that our algorithm still works for the over-parameterized case
(
Zhuo et al. 2021
;
Ma and Fattahi 2022
;
Luo and Zhang 2022
)
where the algorithmic rank
r
is larger than the true rank of
{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
, a thorough theoretical investigation is beyond the scope of this paper. Convex methods such as
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
and
Belloni et al. 2011
need no initialization or the pre-requisite of rank. But our primary interest is to study both the computational and statistical issues in robust estimation. Our algorithms (i.e., the phase two stepsize) also need the noise level
\gamma
, which is not required by the convex approaches based on the square-root LASSO or the nuclear-norm penalized absolute loss
(
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
;
Belloni et al. 2011
;
Minsker et al. 2022
)
. Fortunately,
\gamma
can be reliably estimated by our methods. The phase one iterations terminate until
\sum_{i=1}^{n}|Y_{i}-\langle{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}},{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\rangle|\asymp\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\xi_{i}|\asymp n\gamma
where
l_{1}
is the number of iterations in phase one. Then the phase two stepsize can be taken as
\eta=c\cdot f({\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}})/n^{2}
for some tuning parameter
c>0
. In simulation experiments, we observe that the algorithm is very tolerant with the selection of
c
.

Determine the phase

Due to the geometrically decay of stepsizes during phase one, after some iterations, the stepsize will enter the level
O(\mu_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}L_{\textsf{\tiny stat}}^{-2})
desired by the phase two convergence. On the other hand, if the phase one iterations continue, the stepsize will diminish fast and the value of objective function becomes stable. This is indeed observed in numerical experiments. See Figure
3
and Figure
4
in Section
7
. Therefore, once the stepsize falls below a pre-chosen small threshold, the phase two iterations can be initiated and the stepsizes are fixed afterwards until convergence.

6
Huber’s Contamination Model

The robustness against outliers is often studied for the Huber’s
\varepsilon
-contamination model
(
Huber and Ronchetti 2009
;
Chen et al. 2016
;
Chen et al. 2018
;
Diakonikolas and Kane 2019
)
, which assumes the data are i.i.d. with a common distribution
d\,P=(1-\epsilon)\,d\,P_{i}+\epsilon\,d\,P_{o}
where
P_{i}
denotes the distribution of inliers,
P_{o}
is an arbitrary distribution of outliers, and
\epsilon\in[0,1)
. We now demonstrate the robustness of our algorithms against outliers. Without loss of generality, throughout this section, we assume
\epsilon
-fraction of the responses
\{Y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
are corrupted with outliers. Denote the indices of outliers as
{\mathcal{O}}
and inliers as
{\mathcal{I}}
where
{\mathcal{O}}\cup{\mathcal{I}}=\{1,\dots,n\}
and
|{\mathcal{O}}|=\lceil\epsilon n\rceil
. Basically,
({\mathbf{X}}_{i},Y_{i})
satisfies the linear model (
1
) for
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
. For outliers
i\in{\mathcal{O}}
, the response
Y_{i}
is arbitrarily corrupted. Note that we still assume all the covariates
{\mathbf{X}}_{i}
’s or
vec({\mathbf{X}}_{i})
’s satisfy the Assumption
1
. Interested readers are suggested to refer to
Depersin 2020
;
Lecué and Lerasle 2019
;
Chinot et al. 2020
;
Thompson 2020
;
Diakonikolas et al. 2019
for more general settings of Huber’s
\epsilon
-contamination model. Due to page limit, we only consider the absolute loss.

6.1
Sparse linear regression

Using the same notations from Section
3
, the following theorem describes the statistical guarantees of the IHT-
\ell_{1}
algorithm under Huber’s
\epsilon
-contamination model.

Theorem 7
.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds and
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}
satisfy Assumption
2
. There exist absolute constants
C_{1},\dots,C_{6}>0
,
c_{2}^{*}\in(0,1)
such that if
n\geq C_{1}C_{{\tiny u}}C_{{\tiny l}}^{-1}\tilde{s}\log(2d/\tilde{s})
,
\tilde{s}\geq C_{2}(C_{{\tiny u}}/C_{{\tiny l}})^{8}(b_{0}/b_{1})^{8}\cdot s
, the corruption rate
\epsilon\leq C_{{\tiny l}}^{1/2}(4C_{{\tiny l}}^{1/2}+C_{{\tiny u}}^{1/2})^{-1}
, the initial stepsize
\eta_{0}\in(nC_{{\tiny u}})^{-1}C_{{\tiny l}}^{1/2}\cdot\allowbreak\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|_{2}\cdot\left[1/8,\ 3/8\right]
, the phase one stepsize
\eta_{l}=(1-c_{1})^{l}\eta_{0}
with any fixed constant
c_{1}\leq C_{{\tiny l}}C_{{\tiny u}}^{-1}/64
, and the phase two stepsize
\eta_{l}=\eta\in C_{{\tiny l}}^{1/2}b_{1}^{2}(nb_{0}C_{{\tiny u}})^{-1}\cdot\left[C_{5},C_{6}\right]
, then with probability over
1-2\exp(-C_{3}\tilde{s}\log(2d/\tilde{s}))-3\exp(-(n\log(2d/\tilde{s}))^{1/2}\log^{-1}n)
, after at most
O\big(\log(\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|_{2}/\gamma)+\log(n\gamma b_{0}^{-1}\log(2d/\tilde{s}))\wedge\log(\gamma/b_{0}\epsilon)\big)
iterations, Algorithm
1
outputs an estimator with the error rate

\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|_{2}\leq\frac{C_{4}C_{{\tiny u}}^{1/2}}{C_{{\tiny l}}}\cdot b_{0}\cdot\max\left\{\Big(\frac{\tilde{s}\log(2d/\tilde{s})}{n}\Big)^{1/2},\epsilon\right\}.

Our theorem allows
\epsilon
to be any value smaller than
C_{{\tiny l}}^{1/2}(4C_{{\tiny l}}^{1/2}+C_{{\tiny u}}^{1/2})^{-1}
.
When the noise of inliers is Gaussian, the error rate matches with the minimax lower bound
(
Chen et al. 2016
;
Chen et al. 2018
)
. The rates derived by
Chinot et al. 2020
;
Depersin 2020
have an
O(\epsilon^{1/2})
term and the rate by
Thompson 2020
has an
O(\epsilon\log(1/\epsilon))
term.

6.2
Low-rank linear regression

Using the same notations from Section
4
, the following theorem describes the statistical guarantees of the RsGrad algorithm under Huber’s
\epsilon
-contamination model.

Theorem 8
.

Suppose that
\{{\rm vec}({\mathbf{X}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}
satisfy Assumptions
1
and
2
, respectively. There exist constants
c_{0}^{\ast},c_{1},c_{2}^{\ast}\in(0,1),C_{1}^{\ast},C_{2},C_{3}>0
such that if the sample size
n\geq C_{3}C_{{\tiny u}}C_{{\tiny l}}^{-1}d_{1}r
, the corruption rate
\epsilon\leq C_{{\tiny l}}^{1/2}(4C_{{\tiny l}}^{1/2}+C_{{\tiny u}}^{1/2})^{-1}
, the initialization
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{0}^{\ast}\sigma_{r}
, the initial stepsize
\eta_{0}\in C_{{\tiny l}}^{1/2}(16nC_{{\tiny u}})^{-1}\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}-{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\cdot\left[0.2,0.3\right]
, the phase one stepsize
\eta_{l}=(1-c_{3})^{l}\eta_{0}
with any fixed constant
c_{3}\leq c_{1}
, and the phase two stepsize
\eta_{l}=\eta\asymp C_{{\tiny l}}C_{{\tiny u}}^{-2}b_{1}^{2}(nb_{0})^{-1}
, then with probability at least
1-\exp(-c_{1}rd_{1})-3\exp(-\sqrt{n}/\log n)
, after at most
O\big(\log(\sigma_{r}/\gamma)+C_{1}^{\ast}\log\big(\gamma n/(d_{1}rb_{0})\big)\wedge\log(\gamma/b_{0}\epsilon)\big)
iterations, the Algorithm
2
outputs an estimator with the error rate

\|\hat{\mathbf{M}}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq\frac{C_{2}C_{{\tiny u}}}{C_{{\tiny l}}^{2}}\cdot b_{0}^{2}\cdot\max\left\{\frac{d_{1}r}{n},\epsilon^{2}\right\}.

Exact recovery under sparse corruptions has been studied by
Li et al. 2020
.
Tong et al. 2021b
derived sub-optimal rates under sub-Gaussian noise and sparse corruptions. Our method can deal with heavy-tailed noise and is outlier-robust and minimax optimal.

7
Numerical Experiments

This section presents the numerical simulation results to showcase the two-phase convergence phenomena and compare the attained accuracy with the existing algorithms. Due to page limit, some results are relegated to the supplement.

7.1
Sparse linear regression

Our algorithm is referred to as the IHT-
\ell_{1}
. We compare with with the
adaptive Huber
method from
Sun et al. 2020
and the
truncation
method from
Fan et al. 2021
. The algorithmic parameters of adaptive Huber and truncation methods are fine-tuned for their best performances. Note that our theoretical analysis of IHT-
\ell_{1}
can only treat the case
\tilde{s}>s
because of technical difficulties. The simulation results show that ITH-
\ell_{1}
still performs well when setting
\tilde{s}=s
. The two-phase convergence dynamic of ITH-
\ell_{1}
is similar to that of the low-rank linear regression and is thus skipped.

Accuracy

We set
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}=(16,4,1,0,\dots,0)^{\top}
where
d=50
and
s=3
. The covariate vectors
\{{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
have i.i.d.
N(0,1)
entries. The sample sizes are chosen from
n\in\{50,300\}
. The noise can be Gaussian or have a Student’s t distribution with d.f.
\nu=2
. Figure
2
presents the box-plot of
\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\ast}\|_{2}
based on 50 simulations.

7.2
Low-rank linear regression

We now showcase the two-phase convergence of RsGrad and compare the achieved accuracy with the
convex-huber
,
convex-
\ell_{1}

(
Elsener and van de Geer 2018
)
,
convex-
\ell_{2}

(
Candes and Plan 2011
)
, and the (non-convex) ScaledSM methods
(
Tong et al. 2021b
)
. The accuracy comparison is deferred to the supplement. For ScaledSM and the phase one iterations of RsGrad, the stepsizes are set as
\eta_{0}=c_{1}\|{\mathbf{M}}_{0}\|n^{-1}
and
\eta_{l}=q\cdot\eta_{l-1}
where
c_{1}>0
,
0<q<1
are parameters to be tuned and
\|\cdot\|
denotes the matrix operator norm. As
Tong et al. 2021b
suggested, we choose
q=0.91
. For the phase two iterations of RsGrad, we set the constant stepsize at
\eta_{l}=c_{2}{\mathbb{E}}|\xi|n^{-1}
where
c_{2}>0
is a tuning parameter. Here we assume
{\mathbb{E}}|\xi|
is known for simplicity. In practice,
{\mathbb{E}}|\xi|
can be estimated by
n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|Y_{i}-\langle{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}},{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\rangle|
where
{\mathbf{M}}_{l_{1}}
is the output after phase one iterations. Following
Tong et al. 2021b
, denote the signal-to-noise ratio
\text{SNR}:=20\log_{10}\big(\|{\mathbf{M}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}/{\mathbb{E}}|\xi|\big)
. The relative error is calculated by
\|{\mathbf{M}}_{l}-{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\|{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{-1}
.

Two-phase convergence

We compare the convergence dynamics between RsGrad and ScaledSM. We experiment RsGrad with the absolute loss (
RsGrad-
\ell_{1}
) and Huber loss (
RsGrad-Huber
), but ScaledSM was proposed only for the absolute loss. The dimensions and ranks are fixed at
d_{1}=d_{2}=80
and rank
r=5
, respectively. The phase one iterations are terminated when the stepsize becomes rather small, e.g., smaller than
10^{-10}
, and then the phase two iterations are initiated.

The first set of simulations is to showcase the convergence performance under Gaussian noise. The SNR is set at
\{40,80\}
and the sample sizes are varied among
\{2.5,5\}\times rd_{1}
. The convergence dynamics are displayed in Figure
3
, which clearly show a two-phase convergence of RsGrad. We note that ScaledSM performs poorly when sample size is small (
n=2.5rd_{1}
). It is possibly due to the instability of inverse scaling under a small sample size. The bottom two plots in Figure
3
show that, after the phase one iterations, RsGrad achieves a similar perfomrance as ScaledSM. However, with a second phase convergence, RsGrad (using either Huber or absolute loss) eventually delivers a more accurate estimate than ScaledSM.

(a)

SNR=40

(b)

SNR=80

(c)

SNR=40

(d)

SNR=80

:
Convergence dynamics of RsGrad and ScaledSM
(
Tong et al. 2021b
)
under Gaussian noise. Top two figures:
n=2.5rd_{1}
; bottom two figures:
n=5rd_{1}
.

The second set of simulation is to test the convergence performance under heavy-tailed noise. For simplicity, the noise is sampled independently from a Student’s
t
-distribution with
{\rm d.f.}

\nu=2
, which has a finite first (absolute) moment and an infinite second moment. Other parameters are selected the same as in the Gaussian case. Figure
4
presents the convergence performance showing that both RsGrad (either absolute loss or Huber loss) and ScaledSM are robust to heavy-tailed noise.
Similarly, we can observe the two-phase convergence of RsGrad whose second phase iterations deliver a more accurate estimate than ScaledSM, especially when SNR is large.

. Top two figures:
